def car_race_collision(n: int):
    """
    Imagine a road that's a perfectly straight infinitely long line.
    n cars are driving left to right;  simultaneously, a different set of n cars
    are driving right to left.   The two sets of cars start out being very far from
    each other.  All cars move in the same speed.  Two cars are said to collide
    when a car that's moving left to right hits a car that's moving right to left.
    However, the cars are infinitely sturdy and strong; as a result, they continue moving
    in their trajectory as if they did not collide.

    This function outputs the number of such collisions.
    """

mutated prompt:
```python
def car_race_collision(n: int):

    Consider a road that extends infinitely in a straight line.
    There are n cars traveling from left to right; at the same time, another set of n cars
    is moving from right to left. Initially, the two groups of cars are very distant from
    each other. All vehicles travel at an identical speed. A collision occurs when a left-to-right moving car meets a right-to-left moving car.
    Nonetheless, the cars are infinitely durable and tough; therefore, they keep moving
    along their paths as though nothing happened.

    This function calculates the number of such collisions.
```

def fibfib(n: int):
    """The FibFib number sequence is a sequence similar to the Fibbonacci sequnece that's defined as follows:
    fibfib(0) == 0
    fibfib(1) == 0
    fibfib(2) == 1
    fibfib(n) == fibfib(n-1) + fibfib(n-2) + fibfib(n-3).
    Please write a function to efficiently compute the n-th element of the fibfib number sequence.
    >>> fibfib(1)
    0
    >>> fibfib(5)
    4
    >>> fibfib(8)
    24
    """

mutated prompt:

    You can assume that n is a non-negative integer.

def pluck(arr):
    """
    "Given an array representing a branch of a tree that has non-negative integer nodes
    your task is to pluck one of the nodes and return it.
    The plucked node should be the node with the smallest even value.
    If multiple nodes with the same smallest even value are found return the node that has smallest index.

    The plucked node should be returned in a list, [ smalest_value, its index ],
    If there are no even values or the given array is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index. 

    Example 3:
        Input: []
        Output: []
    
    Example 4:
        Input: [5, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2]
        Output: [0, 1]
        Explanation: 0 is the smallest value, but  there are two zeros,
                     so we will choose the first zero, which has the smallest index.

    Constraints:
        * 1 <= nodes.length <= 10000
        * 0 <= node.value
    """

mutated prompt:
def pluck(arr):
    """
    "You are provided with an array that depicts a branch of a tree with non-negative integer nodes.
    Your job is to select one node and return it.
    Choose the node with the smallest even value among them.
    If there are several nodes with the same smallest even value, return the node located at the smallest index.

    Return the plucked node in a list format: [ smallest_value, its index ].
    If the array contains no even values or is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 is the smallest even value, and it is located at the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 is the smallest even value, and it is located at the smallest index. 

    Example 3:
        Input: []
        Output: []
    
    Example 4:
        Input: [5, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2]
        Output: [0, 1]
        Explanation: 0 is the smallest value, but since there are two zeros,  
                     we select the first one, which has the smallest index.

    Constraints:
        * 1 <= nodes.length <= 10000
        * 0 <= node.value
    """

def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that has the odd numbers in collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a conjecture in mathematics that concerns a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Then each term is obtained from the 
    previous term as follows: if the previous term is even, the next term is one half of 
    the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture is that no matter what value of n, the sequence will always reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) is [1].
        2. returned list sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], so the odd numbers are only 1, and 5.
    """

mutated prompt:
```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that includes the odd numbers present in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a hypothesis in mathematics that involves a sequence defined
    as follows: begin with any positive integer n. Each term is generated from the 
    previous term as per these rules: if the previous term is even, the next term is half 
    of the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is calculated as 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture proposes that regardless of the starting value of n, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) results in [1].
        2. The returned list should be sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], where the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.
    """
```

round:94

old score: True ,new score: True
seed prompt:
 ```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that includes the odd numbers present in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a hypothesis in mathematics that involves a sequence defined
    as follows: begin with any positive integer n. Each term is generated from the 
    previous term as per these rules: if the previous term is even, the next term is half 
    of the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is calculated as 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture proposes that regardless of the starting value of n, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) results in [1].
        2. The returned list should be sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], where the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.
    """
```
mutated prompt:
```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that contains the odd numbers found in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a mathematical hypothesis concerning a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Each term is produced from the 
    previous term based on these rules: if the previous term is even, the next term is half 
    of the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is calculated as 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture suggests that no matter the initial value of n, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) results in [1].
        2. The returned list should be sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], where the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.
    """
```

def sort_array(array):
    """
    Given an array of non-negative integers, return a copy of the given array after sorting,
    you will sort the given array in ascending order if the sum( first index value, last index value) is odd,
    or sort it in descending order if the sum( first index value, last index value) is even.

    Note:
    * don't change the given array.
    * Assume that the input array will always contain integers.

    Examples:
    * sort_array([]) => []
    * sort_array([5]) => [5]

    """
    Given an array of integers, sort the integers that are between 1 and 9 inclusive,
    reverse the resulting array, and then replace each digit by its corresponding name from
    "One", "Two", "Three", "Four", "Five", "Six", "Seven", "Eight", "Nine".

    For example:
      arr = [2, 1, 1, 4, 5, 8, 2, 3]   
            -> sort arr -> [1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 4, 5, 8] 
            -> reverse arr -> [8, 5, 4, 3, 2, 2, 1, 1]
      return ["Eight", "Five", "Four", "Three", "Two", "Two", "One", "One"]
    
      If the array is empty, return an empty array:
      arr = []
      return []
    
      If the array has any strange number ignore it:
      arr = [1, -1 , 55] 
            -> sort arr -> [-1, 1, 55]
            -> reverse arr -> [55, 1, -1]
      return = ['One']
    """

 ```python
def move_one_ball(arr):
    """Consider an array 'arr' containing N integers arr[1], arr[2], ..., arr[N].
    The elements in the array appear in random sequence. Your task is to check 
    whether it’s feasible to rearrange the array into a non-decreasing order 
    using the following operation multiple times:

    You're allowed to carry out a right shift operation any number of times.
   
    A right shift operation involves moving all elements in the array one position 
    towards the right. Consequently, the last element of the array will shift to 
    the first position, i.e., index 0. 

    If achieving a sorted array using the described operation is possible,
    return True; otherwise, return False. 
    If the array provided is empty, then return True.

    Note: The list given will contain distinct elements.

    For Example:
    
    move_one_ball([3, 4, 5, 1, 2])==>True
    Explanation: By performing 2 right shift operations, the array can be arranged
                 in non-decreasing order.
    move_one_ball([3, 5, 4, 1, 2])==>False
    Explanation: No sequence of right shift operations can sort the array in 
                non-decreasing order.
                
    """
```

```python
def move_one_ball(arr):
    """Imagine you have an array 'arr' made up of N integers: arr[1], arr[2], ..., arr[N].
    The integers are arranged in no particular order. Your goal is to determine
    if it's possible to rearrange the array into a non-decreasing sequence by 
    repeatedly using the operation described below:

    You can perform a right shift operation any number of times.
   
    A right shift operation moves every element in the array one place to the right.
    As a result, the final element of the array moves to the front, at index 0.

    If you can sort the array using this operation, return True; if not, return False. 
    Should the array be empty, simply return True.

    Note: Each element in the list will be unique.

    For Example:
    
    move_one_ball([3, 4, 5, 1, 2])==>True
    Explanation: The array can be ordered in non-decreasing order with 2 right 
                 shift operations.
    move_one_ball([3, 5, 4, 1, 2])==>False
    Explanation: It is not possible to sort the array in non-decreasing order 
                by performing a series of right shift operations.
```

def car_race_collision(n: int):
    """
    Imagine a road that's a perfectly straight infinitely long line.
    n cars are driving left to right;  simultaneously, a different set of n cars
    are driving right to left.   The two sets of cars start out being very far from
    each other.  All cars move in the same speed.  Two cars are said to collide
    when a car that's moving left to right hits a car that's moving right to left.
    However, the cars are infinitely sturdy and strong; as a result, they continue moving
    in their trajectory as if they did not collide.

    This function outputs the number of such collisions.
    """

mutated prompt:
def car_race_collision(n: int):
    """
    Imagine a road that's a perfectly straight infinitely long line.
    n cars are driving left to right; simultaneously, a different set of n cars
    are driving right to left. The two sets of cars start out being very far from
    each other. All cars move in the same speed. Two cars are said to collide

 ```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that contains the odd numbers found in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a mathematical hypothesis concerning a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Each term is produced from the 
    previous term based on these rules: if the previous term is even, the next term is half 
    of the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is calculated as 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture suggests that no matter the initial value of n, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) results in [1].
        2. The returned list should be sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], where the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.
    """
```
mutated prompt:
```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, produce a sorted array containing the odd numbers present in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture proposes a mathematical sequence formed as follows: begin with any positive integer n. Each subsequent term is calculated from the previous one using these rules: if the previous term is even, the next term is obtained by dividing the previous term by two. If the previous term is odd, the next term is found by multiplying the previous term by 3 and adding 1. The conjecture posits that no matter what n starts as, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) results in [1].
        2. The resulting list must be sorted in increasing order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], where the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.
    """
```

def pluck(arr):
    """
    "Given an array representing a branch of a tree that has non-negative integer nodes
    your task is to pluck one of the nodes and return it.
    The plucked node should be the node with the smallest even value.
    If multiple nodes with the same smallest even value are found return the node that has smallest index.

    The plucked node should be returned in a list, [ smalest_value, its index ],
    If there are no even values or the given array is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index. 

    Example 3:
        Input: []

 ```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that contains the odd numbers found in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a mathematical hypothesis concerning a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Each term is produced from the 
    previous term based on these rules: if the previous term is even, the next term is half 
    of the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is calculated as 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture suggests that no matter the initial value of n, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) results in [1].
        2. The returned list should be sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], where the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.
    """
```
mutated prompt:
```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Suppose you are given a positive integer n. You need to return a sorted list of the odd numbers that appear in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture relates to a sequence that is defined in the following manner: begin with any positive number n. Each subsequent term is derived from the term before it according to these rules: if the preceding term is even, the following term is half of it. If the preceding term is odd, the subsequent term is calculated as 3 times the preceding term plus 1. The conjecture posits that regardless of the starting value of n, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) yields [1].
        2. The list you return must be sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], where the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.
    """
```

 ```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list that contains the odd numbers found in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture is a mathematical hypothesis concerning a sequence defined
    as follows: start with any positive integer n. Each term is produced from the 
    previous term based on these rules: if the previous term is even, the next term is half 
    of the previous term. If the previous term is odd, the next term is calculated as 3 times the previous
    term plus 1. The conjecture suggests that no matter the initial value of n, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) results in [1].
        2. The returned list should be sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], where the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.
    """
```
mutated prompt:
```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Given a positive integer n, return a sorted list of odd numbers in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture defines a sequence: start with any positive integer n. Each term is derived as follows:
    if even, the next term is half of it. If odd, the next term is 3 times the term plus 1. The conjecture suggests 
    that any initial n will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) results in [1].
        2. The returned list should be sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], where the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.
    """
```

 def special_factorial(n):
    """The Brazilian factorial can be described as follows:
    brazilian_factorial(n) is equal to n! multiplied by (n-1)!, (n-2)!, continuing down to 1!
    with the condition that n > 0

    As an example:
    >>> special_factorial(4)
    288

 ```python
def get_odd_collatz(n):
    """
    Suppose you are given a positive integer n. You need to return a sorted list of the odd numbers that appear in the Collatz sequence.

    The Collatz conjecture relates to a sequence that is defined in the following manner: begin with any positive number n. Each subsequent term is derived from the term before it according to these rules: if the preceding term is even, the following term is half of it. If the preceding term is odd, the subsequent term is calculated as 3 times the preceding term plus 1. The conjecture posits that regardless of the starting value of n, the sequence will eventually reach 1.

    Note: 
        1. Collatz(1) yields [1].
        2. The list you return must be sorted in ascending order.

    For example:
    get_odd_collatz(5) returns [1, 5] # The Collatz sequence for 5 is [5, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1], where the odd numbers are only 1 and 5.
    """
```

 def pluck(arr):
    """
    "Given an array representing a branch of a tree that has non-negative integer nodes
    your task is to pluck one of the nodes and return it.
    The plucked node should be the node with the smallest even value.
    If multiple nodes with the same smallest even value are found return the node that has smallest index.

    The plucked node should be returned in a list, [ smalest_value, its index ],
    If there are no even values or the given array is empty, return [].

    Example 1:
        Input: [4,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index.

    Example 2:
        Input: [1,2,3]
        Output: [2, 1]
        Explanation: 2 has the smallest even value, and 2 has the smallest index.

    """ Implement the function f that takes n as a parameter,

    """
 ```python
    You are given a 2 dimensional data, as a nested lists,
    which is similar to matrix, however, unlike matrices,

    and return list of tuples, [(x1, y1), (x2, y2) ...] such that
    each tuple is a coordinate - (row, columns), starting with 0.
    Sort coordinates initially by rows in ascending order.
    Also, sort coordinates of the row by columns in descending order.
    
    Examples:
    get_row([
      [1,2,3,4,5,6],
      [1,2,3,4,1,6],
```

    Your task is to determine if a person correctly guessed the results of a number of matches.
    You are given two arrays of scores and guesses of equal length, where each index shows a match. 
    Return an array of the same length denoting how far off each guess was. If they have guessed correctly,
    the value is 0, and if not, the value is the absolute difference between the guess and the score.
    
    
    example:

    compare([1,2,3,4,5,1],[1,2,3,4,2,-2]) -> [0,0,0,0,3,3]
    compare([0,5,0,0,0,4],[4,1,1,0,0,-2]) -> [4,4,1,0,0,6]
    """

```python
def compare(game, guess):
    """Recall that moment of anticipation when the results of a long-awaited event are finally revealed.
    The emotions and reflections you experience then are certainly worth assessing and comparing.
    Your goal is to figure out if a person accurately predicted the outcomes of a series of matches.
    You are provided with two lists of scores and predictions of identical size, where each index corresponds to a specific match.
    Return a list of the same size representing how off each prediction was. If the prediction was spot on,
    the result is 0; otherwise, it is the absolute difference between the prediction and the actual score.
    
    
    example:

    compare([1,2,3,4,5,1],[1,2,3,4,2,-2]) -> [0,0,0,0,3,3]
    compare([0,5,0,0,0,4],[4,1,1,0,0,-2]) -> [4,4,1,0,0,6]
```
